wane.  In  my  judgment,  (and  I  think  I  ought  to  know 
as  much  about  this  matter  as  any  man  in  Kentucky)  we 
wTere  never  enjoying  as  great,  or  greater  prosperity, 
than  about  that  period.  I  think  I  am  within  the  bounds 
of  truth,  when  I  say,  that  at  the  time  of  the  Union  the 


404  BIOGRAPHY    OF   BARTON    W.    STONE. 

people  called  Christians,  associated  with  B.  W.  Stone, 
numbered  from  eight  to  ten  thousand,  in  Kentucky.  We 
were  not  on  the  wane  then,  but  greatly  on  the  increase. 
But  it  is  still  a  greater  mistake  to  say  Mr.  Campbell  has 
taken  us  under  his  supervision.  May  the  Lord  grant  us 
a  more  faithful  historian,  than  John  L.  Waller ! 

7.  But  once  more.,  and  we  have  done  with  this  disa- 
greeable subject.  Mr.  Waller  tells  his  readers,  as  a 
matter  of  fact,  —  of  history,  that  David  Purviance 
united  with  the  Shaking  Quakers,  where  he  could  enjoy 
to  the  utmost,  the  liberty  of  dancing,  barking,  jerking, 
&c.  &c.  David  Purviance,  a  Shaking  Quaker !  Do, 
Mr.  Waller,  study  your  subject,  before  you  write  history 
again  ;  especially  the  history  of  those  you  regard  as 
great  errorists:  For  men  are  very  prone  to  take  up  evil 
reports  upon  such,  and  upon  very  insufficient  testimony 
to  believe  them. 

David  Purviance  is  now  about  80  years  old ;  and  one 
of  the  firmest  friends  of  the  Bible  cause.  He  is  a  man 
of  talents,  and  unquestionable  piety.  If  Mr.  Waller 
knew  the  good  old  patriarch,  he  could  not  help  admiring 
his  character.  Since  1803,  he  has  stood  fast  upon  the 
great  protestant  position,  that  "the  Bible,  the  Bible 
alone,  is  the  religion  of  protestants."

GUIZOT'S  GIBBON  —  The  History  of  the  Decline  astd 
Fall  of  the  Roman  Empire  :  By  Edward  Gibbon,  Esq.  A  New  Edition,  revi- 
sed and  corrected  throughout,  preceded  by  a  Preface,  and  accompanied  by  Notes, 
critical  and  historical,  relating  principally  to  the  propagation  of  Christianity :  by 
M.  F.  GUIZOT,  Minister  of  Public  Instruction  for  the  kingdom  of  France.  The 
Preface,  Notes,  and  Corrections,  translated  from  the  French  expressly  for  this  edi- 
tion. With  a  Notice  of  the  Life  and  Character  of  Gibbon,  and  Watson's  Reply  to 
Gibbon.    In  2  vols.  imp.  8vo.,  1073  pp. — sheep,  marble  edges.    Price  $6  00. 

We  have  the  pleasure  of  presenting  the  following  notice  of  Guizot's  Gibbon,  from  the  Committee  of  Selection,  to 
the  patrons  of  the  Historical  Family  Library : — 

The  great  work  of  Gibbon  embraces  a  long  and  interesting  period  of  history,  respecting  which  the  English  reader 
has  few  other  sources  of  information.  The  vast  amount  of  facts  which  it  embodies,  and  their  skillful  exhibition,  will 
always  render  it  a  popular  work. 

This  edition,  with  ample  corrections,  was  first  prepared  in  Paris,  in  1512,  by  M.  Guizot,  who  has  since  so  highly 
distinguished  himself  as  a  minister  of  state,  and  the  head  of  one  of  the  political  parties  in  France. 

Though  this  is  a  work  of  such  acknowledged  importance  and  merit,  it  has  never  yet  appeared  in  an  English  dress. 
The  translations  of  Guizot's  Notes  and  Corrections,  by  a  person  every  way  qualified  for  the  task,  has  greatly  enhanced 
the  value  of  a  work  which,  from  its  first  appearance,  has  been  regarded  as  a  standard  in  English  Historical  Literature. 
Rev.  C.  E.  STOWE,  Prof.  Oriental  Literature,  Lane  Sem'ry. 
Rev.  B.  P.  AYDELOTT,  President  Woodward  College. 
Rev.  S.  W.  LYND,  Pastor  Baptist  Church,  Cincinnati: 
Rev.  Wm.  H.  M'GUFFEY,  late  President  CincinHi  College. 
Rev.  J.  BURT,  late  Editor  Standard.

Rev.  R.  H.  BISHOP,  President  Miami  University. 
Hon.  J.  C.  WRIGHT,  Editor  Cincinnati  Gazette.

THE  FAMILY  MEDICAL  LIBRARY: 

A   TREATISE    OX    THE 

PREVENTION  &  CURE  OF  DISEASES 

By  Regimen  and  Simple  Medicines. 

Revised  and  enlarged,  with  the  addition  of  a  vegetable  materia  medica, 
pointing  out  the  virtues,  preparations,  and  doses  of  our  most  valuable  native 
medical  plants,  and  an  Appendix,  illustrated  with 

100  ENG-RAVTJSTGS,  6  OF  WHICH  ARE  COLORED. 
BY   J.    G.    NORWOOD,    M.    D. 

"Among  the  American  writers  whose  works  have  been  freely  put  in  requisition 
for  this  work,  may  be  mentioned — Doctors  Rush,  Chapman,  Caldwell,  Cook,  Barton, 
James,  Dudley,  Eberle,  Short,  Drake,  Dewees,  Yandell,  Dunglison,  and  Cartwright. 
Also  to  the  labors  of  Cooper,  Abernethy,  Burns,  Mackintosh,  Armstrong,  Johnson, 
Thomas,  and  other  English  writers,  who  stand  at  the  head  of  their  profession  in  their 
own  country.  The  object,  throughout  the  entire  work,  being  to  give  the  history, 
characteristic,  symptoms,  progress,  and  termination  of  all  common  diseases,  in  as 
correct,  simple,  and  intelligible  terms  as  possible,  suited  to  the  capacities  of  all  who 
ought,  in  any  event,  to  undertake  the  treatment  of  a  disease,  in  the  termination  of 
which  human  life  may  be  at  stake." 

The  treatment  pursued  by  the  generality  of  practitioners,  and  found  most  SUC-

"  The  Medical  Family  Library  "  is  before  us,  and  we  have 
given  it  such  a  perusal  as  our  leisure  permitted ;  we 
have  also  handed  it  to  a  medical  friend,  who  pronounces  it 
a  valuable  work  for  the  use  of  families,  and  especially 
where  they  are  not  so  situated  as  to  be  able  at  all  times  to 
command  the  services  of  a  physician  in  cases  of  emergen- 
cy. It  is  the  more  valuable  because  it  is  recent,  and  be 
cause  it  is  western,  for  the  diseases  of  our  own  time  and  lo- 
cality demand  peculiar  remedies.  We  feel  much  confidence 
in  saying  that  some  such  publication  should  be  in  every  in- 
telligent family  ;  and  that  the  price  would  be  often  saved  a 
hundred-fold  by  measures  of  prevention  in  a  few  years. — 
Zanesville  Gazette.

NOTICES    OF    THE 

FAMILY  MEDICAL  LIBRARY 


New  Richmond,  Ohio. 
I  have  examined  the  "Family  Medical  Library,"  com- 
piled by  J.  G.  Norwood,  M.  D.,  and  think  it  a  good  work 
for  families.  J.  T.  JOHNSTON,  M.  D. 

New  Richmond,  Clermont  county,  0. 
Sir — I  have  cursorily  examined  the  "  Family  Medical 
Library,"  compiled  by  J.  G.  Norwood,  M.  D.,  and  so  far 
as  such  an  examination  enables  me  to  judge,  do  recommend 
it  to  the  families  of  my  acquaintance,  as  containing  much 
valuable  information,  and  as  an  antidote  to  quackery. 

J.  G.  ROGERS,  M.  D. 
Red  Oak,  Brown  co.,  O.,  April  27.  1842. 
I  would  respectfully  recommend  to  every  family  of  my 
acquaintance,  to  purchase  the  "  Family  Medical  Library," 
compiled  by  Dr.  Norwood,  as  the  best  antidote  to  quackery 
that  I  have  seen.  G.  NORTON,  M.  D. 

Higginsport,  Ohio,  May  21st,  1842.

ind  acquaintances  as  one  of  the  best  works  of  the  kind  ex-

W.  B.  M'CORMICH,  M.  D. 

Circleville,  Ohio. 
I  have  reviewed,  with  some  attention,  the  "  Family  Med- 
ical Library,"  as  revised  by  J.  G.  Norwood,  M.  D.,  and, 
without  hesitation,  would  say  that,  for  a  work  of  its  title, 
it  is  a  very  valuable  one  to  families  whose  locations  fre- 
quently exclude  them  from  the  physician,  at  a  time  of  their 
greatest  extremity,  as  well  as  supply  them  with  necessary 
medical  aid,  when  pecuniary  ability  may  be  wanting. 

WILLIAM  N.  LUCKEY,  M.  D. 

Germanlown,  June  17, 1842. 
I  would  respectfully  recommend  to  all  my  friends  and 
acquaintances  the  "  Family  Medical  Library,"  compiled  by 
J.  G.  Norwood,  to  be  one  of  the  very  best  works  of  the 
kind  now  extant,  as  it  is  a  work  that  will  do  much  to  put 
down  quackery  and  empyricism,  and  should  be  in  the  pos- 
session of  every  intelligent  family ;  and  I  do  cheerfully 
recommend  it  as  such.  JAS.  COMSTOCK,  M.  D. 

Miamisburgh,  June  17, 1842. 
From  a  cursory  examination  of  the  "Family  Medical 
Library,"  by  J.  G.  Norwood,  I  haye  no  hesitation  in  saying 
that  it  contains  much,  very  much,  valuable  information, 
compiled  from  our  best  medical  authors,  and  if  once  placed 
in  the  hands  of  an  intelligent  community,  will  prove  a 
great  check  to  quackery  and  empyricism. 

S.  H.  SMITH,  M.  D. 

"  Family  Medical  Library,"  by  J.  G.  Norwood,  M.  D. 
We  have  examined  the  work  before  us,  and  are  happy  to 
find  that  it  is  a  good  book— good  in  its  influences — good  in 
its  instructions — good  in  its  effects.  We  do  not  believe  in 
every  man  setting  up  to  be  his  own  doctor.  The  more  he 
reads  this  book  the  better  able  a  man  would  be  to  know, 
and  the  less  confidence  he  would  have  in  a  quack.  Quack- 
ery goes  down  as  knowledge  goes  up.  If  every  man  had 
this  l)OOk,  and  was  well  versed  in  its  contents,  the  quacks 
would  have  very  little  to  do.  If  he  were  seriously  ill,  he 
would  be  desirous  of  having  the  aid  and  the  counsel  of  the 
well  read  and  the  experienced  physician.  Mr.  James  has 
done  a  good  service  to  the  public  in  bringing  out  a  book  so 
useful  in  its  character. — Cincinnati  Western  Morn.  Star. 

"  The  Medical  Family  Library"  is  the  title  of  a  highly 
valuable  work  now  on  our  table.  We  have  given  it  such 
a  perusal  as  our  time  permitted.  It  contains  much  valua- 
Die  information  on  the  subject  of  diseases  and  their. reme- 
dies, diet,  &c.  We  feel  confident  in  saying  that  such  a 
work  should  be  in  the  hands  of  every  family. — Danville, 
Kentucky,  Mercury. 

Red  Oak,  Brown  county,  0.,  May  4,  1842. 
Mr.  John  Martin, — Sir :  having  examined  the  "  Family 
Medical  Library,"  I  think  we  would  do  ourselves  and  fam- 
ilies injustice  not  to  purchase  one,  and  I  do  cheerfully  re- 
tommend  it  to  all  my  neighbors.  JONAS  MANN. 


"  The  Family  Medical  Library."— We  call  the  atten- 
tion of  the  reader  to  a  publication  in  another  column  of  our 
paper  in  reference  to  this  valuable  work.  It  is  highly  spo- 
ken of  by  medical  gentlemen  of  eminence,  who  are  gen- 
erally very  jealous  of  publications  of  this  sort ;  this  is  one 
recommendation.  But  its  greatest  recommendation  con- 
sists in  its  own  intrinsic  merits.  Every  family  should  pos- 
sess it  j  it  might  be  to  them  a  most  profitable  purchase,  as 
being  the  means  of  saving  the  lives  of  some  of  its  mem- 
bers. In  the  absence  of  a  physician  it  might  be  advanta- 
geously consulted,  particularly  in  extreme  cases,  of  sudden 
attack,  or  of  accident,  before  one  could  be  called  in ;  by 
following  the  directions  of  this  book,  much  of  suffering,  if 
not  life  itself  might  be  saved.  Every  family  should  have 
it  j  its  price  is  trivial  compared  with  its  usefulness. — Lick, 
ing  Valley  Register. 

"  Medical  Family  Library."— The  reader's  attention  is 
invited  to  the  advertisement  of  the  above  work  by  the  pub- 
lisher. A  specimen  of  the  book  is  also  before  us,  and  we 
have  given  it  such  a  perusal  as  our  leisure  permitted  j  we 
have  also  handed  it  to  a  medical  friend,  who  pronounces  it 
a  valuable  work  for  the  use  of  families,  and  especially 
where  they  are  not  so  situated  as  to  be  able  at  all  times  to 
command  the  services  of  a  physician  in  cases  of  emergen- 
cy. It  is  the  more  valuable  because  it  is  recent,  and  be- 
cause it  is  western,  for  the  diseases  of  our  own  time  and  lo- 
cality demand  peculiar  remedies.  The  book  may  be 
seen  at  this  office ;  and  we  feel  much  confidence  in  saying 
that  some  such  publication  should  be  in  every  intelligent 
family ;  and  that  the  price  would  be  often  saved  a  hundred- 
fold by  measures  of  prevention  in  a  few  years,  while  the 
prejudices  and  whims  of  ignorance  wouid  give  place  to 
more  liberal  and  correct  views. — ZanesvUle  Gazette. 

"  The  Family  Medical  Library :"  a  treatise  on  the  Pre- 
vention and  Cure  of  Diseases. — This  highly  valuable  work 
is  now  before  us.  It  is  published  by  J.  A.  James,  of  Cin- 
cinnati, and  is  now  offered  to  the  public,  cheaper  than  any 
thing  of  the  kind  heretofore.  The  importance  of  such  a 
work  must  at  once  be  obvious  to  every  one.  Very  few 
have  time  or  opportunity  for  a  regular  course  of  medical 
reading,  and  the  work  that  brings  the  most  information  in 
the  smallest  compass,  if  judiciously  arranged,  is  most  valu- 
able to  the  mass  of  mankind.  Such  we  consider  the  "Fam- 
ily Medical  Library."  It  contains  much  that  is  useful  to 
every  class  of  readers.  Its  anatomical  illustrations  are  par- 
ticularly useful  to  those  who  possess  but  a  limited  know- 
ledge of  that  necessary  branch  of  Medical  Science.  There 
is  no  knowledge  more  important  to  us,  and  none  which  we 
are  more  apt  to  neglect,  than  that  of  our  own  physical  na- 
ture, and  that  which  conduces  most  to  our  own  health  and 
well-being-  The  Medical  Library  will,  in  a  great  measure, 
supply  this  deficiency  in  knowledge.  It  will  do  much  to 
make  every  man  his  own  physician.  No  family  should  be 
long  without  a  copy.— Miami  Valley  Register. 

"  Family  Medical  Library,  illustrated  with  one  hundred 
engravings :  by  J.  G.  Norwood,  M.  D."— This  is  a  new  and 
valuable  work,  recently  published  by  J.  A.  James,  of  Cin- 
cinnati. The  book  contains  nearly  nine  hundred  pages 
8vo.,  and  considering  the  fine  style  in  which  it  is  got  up 
throughout,  is  sold  very  cheap.  That  which  gives  the  work 
its  chief  value  in  this  section  of  the  country,  is  its  revision 
by  a  Western  gentleman  of  deservedly  high  standing  in  his 
profession,  and  its  adaptation  to  Western  diseases.  The 
book  cannot  but  prove  a  valuable  acquisition  to  every  intel- 
ligent family  in  the  community,  and  more  especially  to 
those  for  whom  it  is  not  always  convenient  to  obtain  the 
assistance  of  a  physician. — Madison  Banner. 

I  do  hereby  certify  that  I  was  afflicted  with  piles,  some 
twenty  years,  which  terminated  in  fistula.  Several  physi- 
cians  had  given  my  case  up.  I  obtained  "The  Family 
Medical  Library,"  and  by  its  directions  effected  a  cure, 
which  has,  for  two  years,  been  effectual.  Given  under  my 
hand  and  seal,  this  17th  day  of  August,  1842— Abington 
township,  Wayne  county,  Indiana. 

V)       '  "  JOHN  D.  HARPER.
